# 2601.08111v2_Derandomizing_Matrix_Concentration_Inequalities_from_Free_Probability

## Page 1


Derandomizing Matrix Concentration Inequalities from
Free Probability
Robert Wang∗
Lap Chi Lau†
Hong Zhou‡
Abstract
Recently, sharp matrix concentration inequalities [BBvH23, BvH24] were developed using the theory
of free probability. In this work, we design polynomial time deterministic algorithms to construct out-
comes that satisfy the guarantees of these inequalities. As direct consequences, we obtain polynomial time
deterministic algorithms for the matrix Spencer problem [BJM23] and for constructing near-Ramanujan
graphs. Our proofs show that the concepts and techniques in free probability are useful not only for
mathematical analyses but also for efficient computations.
∗Cheriton School of Computer Science, University of Waterloo, Canada. Email: robert.wang2@uwaterloo.ca
†Cheriton School of Computer Science, University of Waterloo, Canada. Research supported by an NSERC Discovery Grant.
Email: lapchi@uwaterloo.ca.
‡School of Mathematics and Statistics, Fuzhou University, China. Research supported in part by National Key R&D Program
of China, Natural Science Foundation of China, and Fuzhou University startup funding. Email: hong.zhou@fzu.edu.cn.

Introduction
Matrix concentration inequalities [Tro12, Tro15, vH17] consider the sum of independent matrix-valued ran-
dom variables X = P
i Xi and show that its spectral statistics such as its operator norm ∥X∥or the moments
of its eigenvalues tr(Xp)
1
p are close to their expected values. These inequalities have found various important
applications in theoretical computer science and mathematics, such as spectral sparsification of graphs [SS11],
randomized numerical linear algebra [Woo14], analysis of semidefinite programs [AK16], probabilistic con-
structions of expander graphs [Fri08], and many more [Tro15].
Derandomization of these concentration
inequalities lead to deterministic algorithms for these problems and deterministic constructions of pseudo-
random objects [WX08, AK16, MOP20]. From the technical perspective, matrix concentration inequalities
are more challenging to prove than the classical concentration inequalities for real-valued random variables
because of the non-commutative nature of matrices.
Consider a general Gaussian model X = Pn

It is known that both the lower and upper bounds are asymptotically tight: The lower bound is of the correct
order when X has independent entries, such that each Ai is supported on a distinct entry. The upper bound
is of the correct order when X is a diagonal matrix, or more generally when all Ai commute so that they can
be simultaneously diagonalized. The dimension-dependent logarithmic factor between the lower and upper
bounds is suboptimal and undesirable in some applications. This motivates the question of whether there is
a more refined quantity, based on the structure of Ai, that provides a tighter approximation to E[∥X∥].
The progress to this question was initiated by Tropp [Tro18] and significantly advanced by Bandeira, Boedi-
hardjo, and van Handel [BBvH23]. Informally, they demonstrated a new phenomenon that when the matrices
Ai are sufficiently non-commutative, then the logarithmic factor can be removed. To formalize this, [BBvH23]
defined the d2 × d2 covariance matrix Cov(X) where

much larger class of random matrices. The random matrix model is Z = A0 + Pn

Universality: Brailovskaya and van Handel [BvH24] consider the general model X := A0 + Pn
i=1 Zi,
where A0 is a deterministic Hermitian d × d matrix and Z1, . . . , Zn are independent d × d random self-
adjoint matrices with E[Zi] = 0 for 1 ≤i ≤n. They defined the corresponding Gaussian matrix model as
G := A0 + Pm
i=1 giAi for some d × d (deterministic) self-adjoint matrices A1, . . . , Am such that E[G] = E[X]
and Cov(G) = Cov(X). They proved the universality principle that, informally, if each ∥Zi∥is small, then
the spectrum of X is close to that of G such that
E[∥X∥2p] ≈E[∥G∥2p],
E[(zI −X)−1] ≈E[(zI −G)−1],
and spec(X) ≈spec(G).
(13)
This allows them to extend the results in [BBvH23] to the general model, with many applications beyond
the Gaussian model as the general model captures discrete random variables.
An interpolation as in (9) is used to compare the spectral statistic of the Gaussian and the general model, but
the calculations are considerably more involved with several new technical ingredients such as the moment-
cumulant formula, a new trace inequality [BvH24, Proposition 5.1], and Poincare’s lemma.
1.2
Our Results
We derandomize the general results in [BBvH23, BvH24] by designing deterministic polynomial time algo-
rithms to find outcomes that satisfy the guarantees in the probabilistic statements. We show applications
in designing deterministic algorithms for matrix discrepancy, spectral sparsification, and constructions of
expander graphs.

1.2.2
Deterministic Matrix Discrepancy
Theorem 1.1 can be interpreted as a general “partial coloring” result in matrix discrepancy theory.
We
demonstrate that it can be directly applied to obtain a deterministic algorithm for the matrix Spencer
problem, matching the randomized algorithm in [BJM23].
Theorem 1.2 (Deterministic Matrix Spencer). Given n × n symmetric matrices A1, . . . , An with ∥Ai∥≤1
and ∥Ai∥2
F ≤r2 for 1 ≤i ≤n, there is a deterministic polynomial time algorithm to compute a coloring
x : [n] →{−1, 1} such that

In [BJM23], the inequality in (4) was used to lower bound the Gaussian measure of the norm ball K := {x ∈
Rn | ∥Pn
i=1 x(i) · Ai∥≤1}, so that Rothvoss’ result [Rot17] can be applied to obtain a partial coloring. In
Theorem 1.2, we apply Theorem 1.1 to obtain a partial coloring without using Rothvoss’ result, providing a
simpler and more direct approach to matrix discrepancy.
The matrix discrepancy result in [BJM23] has found an interesting application in spectral sparsification
of directed graphs [JSS+25], and Theorem 1.2 implies a deterministic algorithm for this application. See
Section 4.4 for details.

As an application of this result, we give a deterministic analog of the spiked detection model. Given a set
of unit vectors v1, . . . , vn with signal values θ1, . . . , θn > 0, we determinstically construct a pesudorandom
matrix W such that for all j, the maximum eigenvector of θjvjv⊤
j + W has nontrivial correlation with vj
if and only if θj > 1. This is a derandomization of [BCSvH24, Theorem 3.1]. When θj ≤1, the vectors vj
are “efficiently hidden” by W with respect to the spectral detection algorithm (analogous to how a planted
clique of size
√
d is “hidden” by a random graph of d vertices). This application shows that Theorem 1.3
can be used to construct pseudorandom matrices that capture the behavior of not only the eigenvalues of
random matrices but also their eigenvectors. See Section 5.4 for details.
1.2.4
General Model
Let X := A0 + Pn
i=1 Zi, where A0 is a deterministic Hermitian d × d matrix and Z1, . . . , Zn are independent
d × d random self-adjoint matrices with E[Zi] = 0 for 1 ≤i ≤n. For derandomization, we assume that each
Zi has discrete support so that it can be enumerated efficiently, which is satisfied in random graph models.
The following are two related models. Let G := A0 + Pm
i=1 giAi be the corresponding Gaussian model of
X with E[G] = E[X] and Cov(G) = Cov(X). Note that the distribution of G is uniquely defined but the
representation as A0 + Pm
i=1 giAi is not unique. Let Xfree := A0 ⊗1 + Pm
i=1 Ai ⊗si be the corresponding
free model of G as defined in (5), where s1, . . . , sm is a free semicircular family and 1 is the identity element.

1.2.5
Deterministic Expander Constructions
Brailovskaya and van Handel [BvH24, Section 3.2] showed that their results for the general model can
be applied to give new probabilistic constructions of expander graphs.
Our deterministic algorithms in
Theorem 1.4 and Theorem 1.5 imply the following consequences:
1. (Edge-Signing Model, Theorem 7.5): For any k-regular graph G = (V, E), with k ≥polylog |V |, there
is a deterministic algorithm to find an edge signing of G whose signed adjacency matrix has eigenvalues
bounded by 2
√
k · (1 + ˜O(k−1
6 )).
2. (Permutation Model, Theorem 7.6): For any k ≥polylog d, there is a deterministic algorithm to
compute 2k perfect matchings over d vertices such that their union has spectral radius 2
√
2k · (1 +
˜O(k−1
6 )).
3. (Lift Model, Theorem 7.12): Let G = (V, E) be a simple (possibly nonregular) graph with maximum
degree kmax ≥polylog(|V |). There is a deterministic algorithm to compute an m-lift of G whose new
eigenvalues are bounded by λ · (1 + ˜O(k
−1
6
max)) where λ is the spectral radius of the universal cover of G.
4. (Group-Labeled Lift Models, Theorem 7.17): Let G = (V, E) be a k-regular simple graph and Γ be
a finite group with k ≥polylog(|Γ|, |V |). There is a deterministic algorithm to compute a Γ-lift of G
whose new eigenvalues are bounded by 2
√
k · (1 + ˜O(k)−1
6 )
To our knowledge, these are the first polynomial time deterministic algorithms for all these settings when
k ≥polylog(|V |). See Section 7.2 for background and details.
1.3
Proof Overview
Our proofs show that the concepts and techniques in the theory of free probability are not only useful for
mathematical analyses, but also useful for efficient computations.
A classical technique for derandomization is the method of conditional expectation. Given a set of variables
X1, . . . , Xn, and a function f(x1, . . . , xn), we wish to find an outcome x1, . . . , xn ∈supp(X1, . . . , Xn) such
that f(x1, . . . , xn) ≤E[f(X1, . . . , Xn)]. The method of conditional expectation allows us to find such an out-
come as long as we can compute the expectation of f conditioned on the outcomes of any subset of variables.
However, for moments or norms of general random matrices, this is typically difficult to compute in poly-
nomial time. For example, if X is a d × d random matrix with independent entries, computing E[tr(Xlog d)]
would take dlog d time. Thus, previous derandomizations of matrix concentration inequalities (e.g., [WX08])
use “pessimistic estimators” to estimate the conditional expectation of various spectral statistics of random
matrices. By nature, these cannot recover the sharp matrix concentration results in [BBvH23] and [BvH24],
as these involve inequalities such as Golden-Thompson, which, just like (2), cannot distinguish between the
commutative and non-commutative settings.
6


## Page 10


In this work, we observe that using the “non-crossing” structure in the theory of free probability, the moments
of the free model Xfree can be computed efficiently in polynomial time via a natural recursive formula; see
Section 6 for computational aspects. This suggests the following framework for de-randomizing the new
matrix concentration inequalities: start with the operator Xfree, and at each iteration, replace a “little bit”
of it with a finite-dimensional random matrix. If this random matrix is simple enough (e.g., with pairwise
independent entries), then we can deterministically find an outcome to replace the random matrix. This
method can be viewed as a free version of the method of conditional expectation, where instead of computing
the expectation of a spectral statistic on X, we compute the spectral statistic on the corresponding operator
Xfree, which is a main theme in this work. Our ideas for derandomization are inspired by algorithms for
matrix discrepancy minimization used for spectral sparsification [LRR17, LWZ25].

We derandomize this interpolation process by discretizing the stochastic integral. In particular, we break

At each step t, we deterministically find an

expected potential change is small over a pairwise independent distribution of update vectors, so we only need
to search over polynomially many possibilities for yt. In addition to being easily derandomizable, this method

Free Probability and Gaussian Analysis: To show that the loss and gain terms approximately cancel
each other out, there are two steps. First, we apply Taylor expansion on the potential function to show that
both expressions are dominated by the second order term in the Taylor expansion. From this second order
expansion, using basic properties of free independence, we show that the difference of the loss term and the
gain term is exactly a weighted sum of differences of the form in (10). This provides a better insight on the
subtle step where the expectation is “pushed inside” in [BBvH23]. Once we reached this form, we can reuse
the Gaussian analysis and the linear algebraic arguments proven in [BBvH23] to bound the difference.
To summarize, the Brownian walk method uses techniques developed in discrepancy theory to ensure that the
outcome satisfies box constraints and linear constraints, while the Gaussian and complex analysis developed

1.3.2
Derandomizing General Random Matrix Models by Random Swap
In [BvH24], Brailovskaya and van Handel showed, via interpolation arguments, that the spectral statistics of
the general random matrix model X = A0 + Pn
i=1 Zi is close to those of the corresponding Gaussian matrix
model. Such a method does not directly lead to a polynomial time derandomization scheme for two reasons.
First, as in the previous setting, the expected Gaussian moments are not easy to compute in general. To
address this issue, we will bypass the Gaussian approximation of Z, and instead directly compare the spectral
statistics of Z with those of the free model
Xfree := A0 + X1 + X2 + · · · + Xn,
where X1, . . . , Xn are freely independent that satisfy Id ⊗τ[Xi] = E[Zi] and Cov(Xi) = Cov(Zi) for all
i ∈[n]. This operator Xfree is exactly the free approximation of the Gaussian matrix corresponding to the
general model Z. See Section 7.1 for the construction of this free model.
The second issue is that unlike in the Gaussian model, where we can approximately decompose a Gaussian
vector into the sum of pairwise independent random vectors, the general random matrix model Z admits
no such simple decomposition. Thus, we make the assumption that each Zi is a discrete random matrix
with polynomial support size. This still encapsulates many important applications such as expander graph
constructions. For example, if Z is the signing of the adjacency matrix of a graph, then each Zi corresponds
to the signing of an edge, with only two outcomes.
Random Swap: The non-Gaussian structure of Z makes it difficult to apply the Brownian walk method
to the general matrix model. Our assumption of polynomial support size of each Zi suggests a more direct
application of the method of conditional expectation. Consider the following “random swap” procedure, in
which we randomly selects an index i ∈[n] and perform the swap Xi ←Zi ⊗1. Then, we find an outcome
of Zi that minimizes the desired potential function. For example, if our potential is the 2p-th moment, then
we want to bound the quantity

There are two new technical ingredients in our proofs.
An important step in our analysis is to derive
a “semicircular integration by parts formula” (see Proposition 6.5). This is crucially used to compute the
potential update for our interpolation with the free model, where the formula allows us to replace “dependent”
random variables by freely independent random variables in the Taylor expansion of (14). One can view this
as a replacement of the moment-cumulant formula used in [BvH24] to carry out the interpolation with the
Gaussian model. A technical remark is that we exploited the property that maxi ∥Xi∥is bounded, while it
does not hold in the Gaussian interpolation setting where ∥Gi∥is unbounded.
9


## Page 13


Another technical ingredient is the use of the barrier method, developed by [BSS12], to derandomize the
norm universality result. We analyze the potential function tr ⊗τ((λI −X)−2p) where λ > λmax(X). In
each iteration of the random swap algorithm, we update the barrier λ ←λ + δ and show that the potential
function does not increase. This allows us to bypass the step of having to simultaneously control the resolvent
norm at many points in [BvH24], which was much more challenging than in the Gaussian setting of [BBvH23]
and required proving new concentration inequalities for the general model. Again, we exploited the property
that maxi ∥Xi∥is bounded for the barrier method to be well-defined. These considerations further highlight
the advantages of directly interpolating between Z and the free model in our analysis.
2
Free Probability and Random Matrices
Free probability is a theory for non-commutative random variables, which are often characterized by the
spectral distributions of matrices and operators.
In Section 2.1, we introduce the basic notations and
definitions and some fundamental results in free probability, then we present the connection to random
matrx theory in Section 2.2.
This section is relatively long, as we aim to provide a friendly introduction of free probability with more
background and intuition. Readers who are familiar with free probability could skip ahead and only come
back when necessary.
2.1
An Introduction to Free Probability
Everything in this subsection can be found in [NS06].
We start with the basic definitions of a non-commutative probability space and the distribution of a non-
commutative random variable. Then, we present the concept of free independence, and the free central limit
theorem, which states that the sum of freely independent non-commutative random variables converges to
a semicircular element. Finally, we show the formulas for computing joint distributions using non-crossing
partitions, which is the underlying combinatorial structure that allows for efficient derandomization.
2.1.1
Algebra and Probability Space
Definition 2.1 (Non-Commutative Probability Space). A non-commutative probability space is a pair (A, τ)
where A is an associative unital algebra A over the field C (i.e. containing a multiplicative identity element
1) and τ : A →C is a unital linear functional with τ(1) = 1. The elements a ∈A are called non-commutative
random variables in (A, φ). A non-commutative probability space (A, τ) is called tracial if τ satisfies the trace
property that τ(ab) = τ(ba) for all a, b ∈A.
For the purpose of this paper, the most important example is the space of random matrices.
Example 2.2 (Random Matrices). Let A be the algebra of d × d random matrices where each entry has
finite moments of all orders. Let τ be the expectation functional such that
τ(X) := E[tr(X)] := 1
dE[Tr(X)] := 1
d
d
X
i=1
Xi,i.
Then (A, E tr) is a tracial non-commutative probability space.
10


## Page 14


The space of random matrices, equipped with the conjugate operation and the operator norm, has some
additional structure and is a C∗-probability space.
Definition 2.3 (C∗-Probability Space). Let (A, τ) be a non-commutative probability space. A is called a
∗-algebra if A is equipped with an anti-linear map ∗: A →A such that (a∗)∗= a and (ab)∗= b∗a∗. (A, τ)
is called a ∗-probability space if A is a ∗-algebra, and τ satisfies τ(a∗) = τ(a) and τ(a∗a) ≥0 for all a ∈A.
If A is equipped with a norm ∥·∥such that the topology over A induced by the distance d(x, y) = ∥x −y∥is
complete, A is called a Banach algebra if ∥xy∥≤∥x∥∥y∥for x, y ∈A.
A is called a C∗-algebra if it is a ∗-algebra and a Banach algebra. (A, τ) is called a C∗-probability space if
A is a C∗-algebra and (A, τ) is a non-commutative probability space.
An element a in a ∗-algebra A is called normal if a∗a = aa∗, self-adjoint if a = a∗, unitary if aa∗= a∗a = 1,
and positive if a = b∗b for some b ∈A.
Let the spectrum of an element a be
spec(a) = {λ ∈C : λ1 −a is not invertible}.
A classical and useful result in matrix analysis is the spectral theorem, which says that normal matrices are
orthogonally diagonalizable. Then, for any normal matrix M and any function f : spec(M) →C, one can
uniquely define the matrix f(M) having the same eigenvectors as M with eigenvalues {f(λ) : λ ∈spec(M)}.
Some common examples include
√
M, eM, and |M|. This functional calculus can be generalized to normal
elements in an arbitrary C∗-algebra.
Theorem 2.4 (Functional Calculus and Spectral Mapping Theorem). Let A be a unital C∗-algebra. For
every a ∈A, its spectrum spec(a) is a compact set contained in the unit disc of radius ∥a∥in C.
If a is normal and f : spec(a) →C is a continuous function, then there is a unique element f(a) ∈A with
spec(f(a)) = f(spec(a)). In words, the spectrum of f(a) is equal to the image of the spectrum of a under f.
In particular, ∥f(a)∥= sup{|f(z)| | z ∈spec(a)}.
Note that if a is self-adjoint, then the spectrum is supported in R, so in that case, we can view f as a
real-valued function.
2.1.2
Distributions and Convergence
In classical probability theory, the distribution of compactly supported random variables can be obtained
from its moments via techniques from Fourier analysis. As such, the distributions of such random variables
are often characterized by their moments. In a similar way, the distribution of a non-commutative random
variable in a C∗-probability space is defined by its moments.
Definition 2.5 (Analytical Distribution). Let a be a random variable in a C∗-probability space (A, τ). The
distribution of a is the unique measure µ : C →R+ which satisfies, for all p ∈N,
Z
zpd(µ(z)) = τ(ap).
We can think of the distribution of a as a distribution of a classical random variable Z satisfying E[Zp] =
τ(ap). If a is normal and τ is a faithful trace, then it can be proved that the support of µ is equal to the
spectrum spec(a). Thus, the distribution of Z is exactly the eigenvalue distribution (or empirical spectral
measure) of a, so Z can be thought of as a “uniformly sampled eigenvalue” of a.
This distribution provides a way to compute the norm using the moments.

2.1.3
Free Independence
A central concept in free probability is free independence, a notion of independence for non-commutative
random variables. In the classical setting, if we are given independent random variables X1, . . . , Xn, we can
compute their joint distribution of the random variables from the individual distributions. Free indepen-
dence, similarly, characterizes the joint distribution of a set of freely independent non-commutative random
variables.
Definition 2.9 (Free Independence). Let A1, A2, . . . , An be a set of unital subalgebras in a probability space
(A, τ). We say A1, . . . , An are freely independently from each other if
τ(a1) = · · · = τ(am) = 0
=⇒
τ(a1 · · · am) = 0,
whenever aj ∈Aij for j ∈[m] and neighboring elements are from different subalgebras such that ij ̸= ij+1
for 1 ≤j < m.
Given a set of random variables a1, a2, . . . , an, we say they are freely independent (or just “free”) if the
algebras that they generate are freely independent.
This definition provides an elegant way to compute the joint distribution. For a particular monomial expres-
sion τ(a1a2 · · · an), we can assume that each ai and ai+1 are from different subalgebras by combining them
into the same variable if they are not. Then, we can write each ai as ¯ai + τ(ai) · 1, where ¯ai := ai −τ(ai)1
is centered such that τ(¯ai) = 0. One can then verify that by expanding out everything, we get a polynomial
where every term either evaluates to 0 under τ by Definition 2.9 or is a scalar multiple of 1. The following
are some simple examples.
12


## Page 16


Example 2.10. Let A and A′ be two subalgebras in a probability space that are freely independent. Let
a, a′ ∈A and b, b′ ∈A′. Then,
1. τ(ab) = τ(a)τ(b).
2. τ(aba′) = τ(aa′)τ(b).
3. τ(aba′b′) = τ(aa′)τ(b)τ(b′) + τ(a)τ(a′)τ(bb′) −τ(a)τ(a′)τ(b)τ(b′).
We note that only the last equation is different from the classical commutative setting. In the classical
setting, if a, a′ are independent from b, b′, then E[aba′b′] = E[aa′] · E[bb′]. We will use the last equation in
our proofs.
2.1.4
Free Central Limit Theorem and Semicircular Element
In classical probability, the central limit theorem states that the normalized sum of n identically distributed
independent random variables converges to the Gaussian distribution as n →∞. In free probability, the free
central limit theorem states that the normalized sum of n identically distributed freely independent random
variables converges to the semicircle distribution as n →∞.
Theorem 2.11 (Free Central Limit Theorem). Let a1, a2, . . . , ∈A be a sequence of freely independent,
identically distributed, self-adjoint random variables in a non-commutative probability space (A, τ) such that
τ(ai) = 0 and τ(a2
i ) = 1 for all i. Then the normalized sum sn = (a1+· · ·+an)/√n converges in distribution
to the semicircle distribution such that

Definition 2.12 (Semicircular Element). Let (A, τ) be a non-commutative probability space. An element

In particular, a standard semicircular element has mean τ(s) = 0 and variance τ(s2) = 1.
We note that a standard semicircular element with mean 0 and variance 1 can be interpreted as the free
analog of the standard Gaussian N(0, 1), but an important difference with N(0, 1) is that the semicircular
distribution is compactly supported.
An interesting property of the semicircle distribution is that its odd moments are zero while its even moments
are given by the Catalan numbers.

2.1.5
Joint Distributions and Non-Crossing Partitions
An important aspect of probability theory is the characterization of moments of random variables using the
combinatorics of partitions.
Definition 2.14 (Partitions). Let P[n] be the set of partitions of n elements, and Pk[n] be the set of partitions
of [n] elements where each part has size k. If π = (V1, . . . , Vq) is a partition of [n], we write u ∼π v if there
exists l ∈[q] such that u, v ∈Vl.
The Wick’s formula states that if g1, . . . , gn are jointly Gaussian random variables with E[gi] = 0 for all i,
then their moments are characterized as:
E[g1g2 · · · gn] =

In a non-commutative probability space, one can define “jointly-semicircular” random variables in an analo-
gous manner, with free independence replacing classical independence. This follows from the key fact that
the sum of free semicircular random variables is still semicircular, just as the sums of independent Gaussians
remain Gaussian.
Definition 2.15 (Jointly Semicircular Distribution). A set of semicircular random variables s1, . . . , sn is
jointly semicircular if there exist freely independent semicircular variables s′

An important property of jointly semicircular random variables is that their moments can be characterized
by an analog of (15), but with the restriction that the summation is just over pairings that contain no
crossings.
Definition 2.16 (Non-Crossing Partitions). Given a partition π ∈P[n], we say π has a crossing, if there
exists i < j < k < l such that i ∼π k and j ∼π l. We say π is non-crossing if no such crossings exists. We
let NC[n] denote the set of all non-crossing partitions of [n], and NCm[n] denote the set of all non-crossing
partitions where each part has size m.
Theorem 2.17 (Semicircular Wick Formula). Let (A, τ) be a non-commutative probability space, and
s1, . . . , sn ∈A be a set of random variables. Then s1, . . . , sn are jointly semicircular if and only if

We can see from Theorem 2.17 that the distribution of jointly semicircular s1, . . . sn are fully determined
by the quantities {τ(si)}n
i=1 and {τ(sisj)}i,j∈[n] for i, j ∈[n]. These are exactly the entries of b and M
respectively in Definition 2.15.
The non-crossing partitions have a simple recursive structure similar to that for the Catalan numbers. This
is the key reason that the moments of the free model Xfree can be computed efficiently in polynomial time
using dynamic programming. See Section 6.
So far the semicircular elements are abstract objects, but we note that they can be concretely realized as
infinite-dimensional operators in a Hilbert space.
Theorem 2.18 (Fock Space). There exists a Hilbert space H a linear functional τ : B(H) →C satisfying
τ(1) = 1, and self-adjoint operators {si}i∈N ∈B(H) such that each si is a semicircular element and s1, s2, . . .
are freely independent.
14


## Page 18


The explicit construction of the Fock space is simple and elegant, but the details are not relevant to this
paper. We mention the explicit construction because it is important in the proofs of formulas and bounds
for norms of free objects that we will see in the next subsection. Because of this construction, sometimes we
also call a semicircular element a semicircular operator.
2.2
Random Matrices and Semicircular Matrices
A fundamental result in random matrix theory is Wigner’s semicircle law, which states that the limiting
distribution of the eigenvalues of many random symmetric matrices is the semicircle distribution in Def-
inition 2.12. This motivated Voiculescu to build a connection between the theory of free probability and
random matrix theory.
2.2.1
Gaussian Random Matrices and Semicircular Family
We begin by defining the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble and outlining its relationship to the family of freely
independent semicircular elements. This will allow us to formally define the free model Xfree and study its
relationship to the Gaussian model.
Definition 2.19 (Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE)). A GOE matrix of dimension d can be expressed

this normalization, we have E[tr(G)] = 0 and E[tr(G2)] = 1.
A classical result in random matrix theory is that if G(N) is a Gaussian GOE matrix of dimension N, then

It follows that in the limit as N →∞, the empirical eigenvalue distribution of G(N) tends towards that of a
semicircle distribution in Definition 2.12.
An important result by Voiculescu is that independent GOE matrices are asymptotically freely independent.

2.2.2
Semicircular Matrices
We have seen that a semicircular operator is a good approximation for a large GOE matrix. What should be
the free operator approximation of general Gaussian matrices? The main result of [BBvH23] is that under
certain “intrinsic freeness” conditions, a random matrix with Gaussian entries can be approximated by a
matrix with semicircular elements.
Formally, let (A, τ) be the semicircular algebra, and Md(C) be the set of d × d complex matrices. We
consider the algebra Md(C) ⊗A, which can be represented as the space of d × d matrices whose entries are
elements in A. We say that an operator X ∈Md(C) ⊗A is a “semicircular matrix” if each of its matrix
entries has the semicircular distribution. This is analogous to a Gaussian matrix in the classical setting. By
Definition 2.15, these operators can be characterized as sums of freely independent semicircular elements
with matrix coefficients.
Definition 2.22 (Semicircular Matrix). Let X ∈Md(C) ⊗A. X is called a semicircular matrix if there
exist matrices A0, A1, . . . , An such that X = A0 ⊗1 + Pn
i=1 Ai ⊗si where s1, . . . , sn are freely independent
semicircular elements. X is called centered if A0 = 0.
The main result in [BBvH23], as stated in (12), is that the Gaussian model X = A0 + Pn

g1, . . . , gn are independent Gaussians can be approximated by the free model Xfree = A0 ⊗1 + Pn

where s1, . . . , sn are freely independent semicircular elements. In their approach, Xfree is approximated by

Partial Trace of Matrices and Operators
For random variables with matrix coefficients, we extend τ to the partial trace operator
φ := Id ⊗τ.
This can be viewed abstractly as a special case of an operator-valued conditional expectation, but we just
need some simple properties such as φ[(A ⊗1)(X)(B ⊗1)] = A · φ(X) · B for X ∈Md(C) ⊗A.
The following identity is from [BBvH23, Lemma 5.2], which will be used to approximate the partial trace of
a semicircular matrix.

3
Technical Tools
In this section, we collect some formulas and inequalities that will be used in the proofs. Many of these
were used in [BBvH23, BvH24] for finite-dimensional random matrices. Since we work directly with general
non-commutative random variables in Md(C) ⊗A for derandomization purpose, we need to generalize some
statements in [BBvH23, BvH24] to the infinite-dimensional setting. The proofs are relatively straightforward
by using the finite-dimensional approximation method, but we include them for completeness.
This section is a reference section, which may be skipped or quickly glossed over at the first reading and is
only referred to when some results are needed.
19

3.1
Calculus for Banach Space
In this subsection, we review some basic facts about differentiating and bounding functions on arbitrary
normed (not necessarily finite-dimensional) vector spaces. While these definitions and theorems are stated
abstractly, we will see concrete applications of them in the main results. For more detailed review of this
subject, we refer the reader to [Car71].
Definition 3.1 (Banach Space and Algebra). A Banach space Y is a vector space over C or R equipped
with a norm ∥·∥such that the topology on Y induced by the distance d(x, y) = ∥x −y∥is complete. If Y is
also an associative algebra over the same field (i.e., closed under multiplication) satisfying ∥xy∥≤∥x∥∥y∥,
then Y is a Banach algebra.
A canonical example of a Banach space is the vector space Cn, equipped with the standard L2 norm. A
canonical example of a Banach algebra is the set of linear operators L(Cn; Cn), equipped with the operator
norm.
Definition 3.2 (Linear Maps). Let Y, Z be Banach spaces. Let L(Y ; Z) be the set of linear maps from Y
to Z. Moreover, given Y1, . . . , Yk, Z, let L(Y1, . . . , Yk; Z) be the set of k-linear maps from Y1 × Y2 × · · · × Yk
to Z. Finally, denote L(Y, Y, . . . , Y
|

}
k times
; Z) as Lk(Y ; Z).
3.1.1
Derivatives
Next, we see the definitions of derivatives of functions over Banach spaces and algebras.
Definition 3.3 (Derivative). Let f : U →Z be a continuous function, and U ⊆Y be an open set. Given a
point a ∈U, f is differentiable at a if there exists a linear map L ∈L(Y ; Z) so that
lim

= 0.
The linear map L is the derivative (or Frechet derivative) of f at a, which is denoted by Df(a). The function
f is differentiable everywhere if it is differentiable on an open set around every a ∈U.
Definition 3.4 (Directional Derivative). Given a particular vector h ∈Y , the directional derivative Df(a)h
of f at a with respect to h is the linear functional in L(Y ; R) such that
Df(a)h := lim

To compute derivatives of functions on Banach spaces, we can use the basic rules of calculus, translated to
this general setting.
Fact 3.6 (Chain Rule). Let Y1, Y2, Y3 be Banach spaces and f : Y1 →Y2, g : Y2 →Y3 be continuous
differentiable functions. Let a ∈Y1 and U, V be open sets such that a ∈U ⊆Y1 and f(a) ∈V ⊆Y2. Then,
for any h ∈Y1,
D(g ◦f)(a)h = Dg(f(a)) · Df(a)h.

3.1.2
Higher Order Derivatives
The map Df : U →L(Y ; Z) can be differentiated further. For each a ∈U, the second derivative D2f(a) is
in L(Y ; L(Y ; Z)). We can identify each linear map L ∈L(Y ; L(Y ; Z)) with a bi-linear map in L(Y, Y ; Z) by
writing L(g, h) = (Lg)h. Thus, we can intrepret D2f(a) as an element in L(Y, Y ; Z). It can be shown that
this map is symmetric, meaning that D2f(a)(h, g) = D2f(a)(g, h) for all a (see [Car71, Theorem 5.1.1]).
This is analogous to ∂x∂yf(x, y) = ∂y∂xf(x, y) for functions on R2.
Extending further, we can define the general k-th order derivative of f as a k-linear map as follows.
Definition 3.9 (Higher Order Derivatives). A function f : U →Z for U ⊆Y is k-times differentiable if
f, Df, . . . , Dk−1f are differentiable everywhere in U. For each a ∈U, the k-th order derivative Dkf(a) at a
can be identified with a multi-linear map Lk(Y, Z). Furthermore, this map is symmetric for all a.
Given the definition of higher-order derivatives, we state some formulas that will be useful in the proofs. The
first is the generalized product rule on the product of n functions, which follows from applying the product
rule in Fact 3.7 n times.

differentiable functions. Let Pℓbe the set of permutations of ℓelements. For any X ∈A,

3.2
Gaussian Analysis
In this subsection, we review some key properties of Gaussian random variables, which are crucial in the
analysis of the Gaussian matrix model in [BBvH23].
We start with the basic Gaussian integration-by-parts formula.
Lemma 3.15 (Gaussian Integration-by-Parts). Let g ∼N(0, 1) and f be a smooth function. Then
E[gf(g)] = E[f′(g)].
A key lemma in [BBvH23, Corollary 4.12] is the Gaussian covariance identity. Given a Gaussian vector x
and smooth functions f, h, the identity bounds the quantity
E[f(x) · g(x)] −E[f(x)] · E[h(x)]
.
22


## Page 26


In other words, it measures the extend to which the functions f and g are uncorrelated over the Gaussian
measure. Since this lemma was only stated in the vector setting, we provide a more compact and self-
contained proof of it in the matrix setting for our applications.

Another useful property of Gaussians is that the expectation of convex functions of Gaussians do not increase
under projection. The following lemma will be used in the deterministic algorithm for matrix discrepancy.
23


## Page 27


Lemma 3.17 (Convex Functions of Gaussians). Let y, z ∈Rn be independent and centered Gaussian random
vectors. Let f : Rm →C be a convex function. Then
E[f(y)] ≤E[f(y + z)]
Let g ∼N(0, In) be a standard Gaussian vector and P be an n × n projection matrix such that P 2 = P.
Then
E[f(Pg)] ≤E[f(g)]
Proof. Since y and z are independent and centered Gaussian vectors, it follows that y = E[y + z|y]. Thus,
by applying Jensen’s inequality for conditional expectations,
Ey[f(y)] = Ey[f(E[y + z|y])] ≤EyE[f(y + z)|y] = E[f(y + z)].
For the second inequality, let g and g′ be independent standard Gaussians. Let y = Pg and z = (I −P)g′.
Using P 2 = P and g, g′ ∼N(0, In), we see that E[(y + z)(y + z)⊤] = E[yy⊤+ zz⊤] = I, meaning y + z has
the same distribution as g. Thus, by the first inequality,
E[f(Pg)] = E[f(y)] ≤E[f(y + z)] = E[f(g)].
3.3
Trace Inequalities
Given a C∗-probability space (A, τ), the expectation functional τ naturally defines an Lp norm on A:
∥a∥p := τ(|a|p)

Hölder’s inequality is generalized in this setting: For any p, q > 0 such that 1

(19)
In this paper, we will require several generalized versions of these Hölder-type trace inequalities for the
product of more than two variables. All of the trace inequalities that we use are standard, or have appeared
in [BBvH23] and [BvH24] for finite dimensional random matrices. We will show that they naturally extend
to the algebra Md(C) ⊗A by approximating operators in Md(C) ⊗A with finite dimensional random
matrices, then apply the weak convergence result in Theorem 2.20 by taking limits. We remark that these
trace inequalities can also be proved directly in the infinite dimensional setting by extending A to a Von-
Neumann algebra of operators, but we will use the finite dimensional approximation method instead to avoid
introducing heavier operator-algebraic machinery.

We extend Lemma 3.19 to the infinite dimensional setting. One caveat to note is that the Schatten ∞-norm
of finite dimensional Gaussian matrices are always unbounded, and thus do not converge to the ∞-norm of
semicircular operators. This issue can be handled by first approximating the ∞-norm with a large finite

3.4
Intrinsic Freeness
A key part of the analysis in [BBvH23] is to prove a version of trace Hölder’s inequality that bounds
the crossing terms in the product of random matrices. This underlies a key phenomenon called “intrinsic
freeness”, in which the non-commutative structure of random matrices suppresses the crossing terms between
themselves.

Proposition 3.25 can be extended to z on the real line that is not in the spectrum of X, which will be useful

Corollary 3.26 (Intrinsic Freeness of Resolvents on Reals). Let X ∈Md(C) ⊗A be a self-adjoint semicir-
cular matrix and Y ∈Md(C) be a finitely supported random matrix. Suppose λ ∈R is bounded away from
the spectrum of X, i.e., dist(λ, spec(X)) > 0. Then for any p, q ∈N, p + q ≥4, we have
|E tr ⊗τ(Mλ(X)p · (Y ⊗1) · Mλ(X)q · (Y ⊗1))−E tr(φ[Mλ(X)p] · Y · φ[Mλ(X)q] · Y )|
≲p · q · ˜ν(X)2 · ˜ν(Y )2 · tr ⊗τ(Mλ(X)p+q+2).
Proof. Since λ is bounded away from spec(X), we have Mλ(X) = limδ→0 Mλ+δi(X). Thus, we can apply
Proposition 3.25 with z = λ + δi for arbitrarily small δ > 0. Taking the limit as δ →0 then gives the desired
result.

This provides a one-sided derandomization of Theorem 4.1, with an essentially negligible additional error

In the general case when ε, δ > 0, there is a multiplicative factor loss of Kε,δ on top of the additive loss of
O(p3/4√σν) in Theorem 4.1. In many applications such as for matrix discrepancy, a constant multiplicative
loss is tolerable. In exchange, we can handle additional linear and box constraints (e.g., x ∈[±1]n), which
broadens the scope of where such a bound can be applied.
4.2
Brownian Walk Algorithm
We provided an overview of the algorithm in Section 1.3, using a modified interpolation from X to ¯Xfree and
the Brownian walk approach in algorithmic discrepancy theory to implement the interpolation.
In the algorithm, we use the following potential function to govern the moments of our mixed operators.
Given a vector x ∈Rn, define the potential function5
Φ(t, x) := tr ⊗τ

The idea of the Brownian walk algorithm is to divide the interval [0, 1] into small discrete steps of size η.
We start with t = 0, m = 0, and x = 0. At the m-th step, we update t ←t + η/n and xm+1 ←xm + ym,
where the update ym is chosen so that ∥xm∥2 increases by a small but non-trivial amount, while subject
to the box and linear-subspace constraints required to satisfy conditions (1) and (2) in Theorem 4.2. The
multiplicative factor Kδ,ε ensures that the decrease of the potential function due to the shrinking of the free
part is sufficiently large enough to offset the increase of the potential function caused by the update ym. The
key in the analysis is to control the increase of the potential function.
5The 2/p in the exponent normalizes the potential to be the 4-th power of the 2p-norm. This choice of taking the 4-th power
is only to make calculations more convenient. The analysis would still work if we use 1/2p in the exponent, as we will do later
in Section 7.4.

We first prove Theorem 4.2 by analyzing this algorithm assuming Proposition 4.3, which will then be proved
in the next subsection.
Proof of Theorem 4.2.
First, we argue that Step (2) of the algorithm always succeeds. Since the algorithm
has not terminated, the number of alive variables is at least |Fm| ≥(1 −δ)n. Thus, the dimension of the
(bad) subspace H⊥
m is at most (δ + ε)n + 1, with at most εn constraints from H, at most δn constraints from
{y(i) = 0 for all i ∈Fm}, and one constraint from ⟨y, xm⟩= 0. By Proposition 4.3, there exists a (good)
subspace H′ of dimension at least (ε + δ)n + 2 such that any y ∈H′ with ∥y∥2 = 1 satisfies (24). Therefore,
the subspace H′ ∩Hm is non-empty, and hence Step (2) always succeeds.
Now, we check that each of the three conditions in Theorem 4.2 are satsified. For the box constraints, when
a coordinate is still alive such that x(i) > b(i) + 1
n and x(i) < c(i) −1
n, the choice of η in (23) ensures that
the coordinate will not change by more than 1/n, hence the box constraints will never be violated. For the
linear subspace constraint, each update step y ∈H by Step (1) of the algorithm, and hence each xm ∈H.

4.4
Applications to Matrix Discrepancy
An important tool in discrepancy theory is Rothvoss’s algorithmic partial coloring theorem for convex bodies.
The partial coloring theorem states that if a symmetric convex body has large enough Gaussian volume,
then it contains a point with a constant fraction of entries in ±1. Rothvoss proved a constructive version of
this result using a randomized Gaussian projection algorithm, with wide-ranging applications in algorithmic
discrepancy theory, spectral sparsification, and numerical linear algebra.
Theorem 4.4 (Randomized Partial Coloring for Convex Body [Rot17]). Let ε <
1
60000, δ ≍log 1
ε be constants
and U ⊆Rn be a subspace of dimension at least (1 −δ)n. Let K ⊆Rn be a symmetric, convex set, and
x0 ∈[−1, 1]n. Suppose the Gaussian measure of K is at least e−εn. There is a randomized polynomial time
algorithm to find a vector x satisfying
1. x ∈K,
2. x + x0 ∈[−1, 1]n,
3. at least εn coordinates of x + x0 are in {−1, 1}.
Using Theorem 4.2, we provide a derandomization of Rothvoss’s result when K is the p-norm ball of matrices
(with constant Gaussian measure). The proof is based on the idea in [BJM23] to apply partial coloring on
projected matrices.

4.4.1
Matrix Spencer Problem
In the matrix Spencer problem, we are given n × n matrices A1, . . . , An with ∥Ai∥≤1 and for i ∈[n]. The
goal is to find a coloring x : [n] →[−1, 1] so that ∥A(x)∥≲O(√n). Bansal, Jiang, and Meka [BJM23]
proved that such a coloring exists when each Ai has ∥Ai∥2
F ≤O(n/ log3 n), by using (4) from [BBvH23]
to show that the Gaussian measure of the matrix p-norm ball is large and then applying Rothvoss’ partial
coloring theorem. We use Proposition 4.5 to provide a deterministic polynomial time algorithm with the
same guarantee.

4.4.2
Spectral Sparsification of Eulerian Graphs
Given a graph G = (V, E), a spectral sparsifier is a sparse reweighted subgraph whose Laplacian approximates
that of G. A classical result, due to Batson, Spielman, and Srivastava [BSS12], is that any undirected graph
can be ε−approximated by a sparsifier of size O(n/ε2). Recently, Reiss and Rothvoss [RR20] introduced
a new perspective on spectral sparsification by framing it as a matrix discrepancy problem. This powerful
framework allows the construction of linear-sized spectral sparsifiers satisfying additional constraints, and
was further developed and derandomized in [LWZ25].
One advantage of the discrepancy framework for spectral sparsifciation is that it can be applied to directed
graphs. Given a weighted Eulerian directed graph, ⃗G = (V, E, w), a spectral sparsifier of ⃗G is a sparse
reweighted subgraph, ⃗H, satisfying
L†/2
G (⃗L ⃗G −⃗L ⃗H)L†/2
G
 ≤ε,
where ⃗L ⃗G is the directed Laplacian matrix of ⃗G.
Jambulapati, Sachdeva, Sidford, Tian, and Zhao [JSS+25] combine the matrix discrepancy result from [BJM23]
with an effective resistance decomposition technique to construct a Eulerian sparsifer of size O(ε−2n log n +
ε−4/3n log5/3 n) in randomized polynomial time. Using the deterministic partial coloring result in Theo-
rem 4.2 as a black box, we obtain a deterministic polynomial time algorithm with the same sparsity guarantee
(but with a much slower runtime). We refer the reader to [JSS+25] for details.

5.1
Overview and Outline
In [BBvH23], the concentration of the full spectrum was established by interpolating between the resolvent of
a Gaussian matrix X and the resolvent of the corresponding free model Xfree. In particular, they proved that
for any z ∈C with Im(z) > 0,
(zI −X)−1 ≈
(z1 −Xfree)−1 with high probability. Since the operator
norm is difficult to analyze directly due to its lack of smoothness, this comparison was done indirectly through
the p-norm of the resolvent for p ≈log d. Then, by taking a union bound over z = λ + iε for a range of λ’s
covering the spectrum spec(X), they established that spec(X) ⊆spec(Xfree) + [−ε, ε] with high probability.
[BBvH23] did not establish the other direction that spec(Xfree) ⊆spec(X) + [−ε, ε]. This is because while it
is known that
(zI −X)−1 ≍
(zI −X)−1
2p for p ≍log d, the corresponding inequality was not known for
the free model Xfree. Later, in [BCSvH24], the ultracontractivity bounds in Theorem 3.27 and Theorem 3.28
were established, which implied that
(zI −Xfree)−1 ≍
(zI −Xfree)−1
2p when p ≍log d. This shows
that the two-sided concentration of spectrum result follows directly from the proof in [BBvH23].
In our work, we will also use the resolvent method. As in [BBvH23], we will pick a set of points z = λ + iε,
where λ is a fine cover of spec(X) ∪spec(Xfree). For each such z, we define a potential function Φz(t, x) to
control the quantity
(zI −A(x))−1
2p in terms of
(z1 −Xfree)−1
2p for p ≈log d. To bound the expected
change in each potential function, we will use similar methods as in Section 4.3, where we use second-order
Taylor approximation to express the potential change in terms of the crossing terms, which will then be
bounded using the intrinsic freeness of resolvents in Proposition 3.25. This can be seen as a derandomization
of [BBvH23, Theorem 6.1].
To control all potential functions simultaneously and deterministically, we define an aggregate potential
function that combines all of the resolvent potential functions. By controlling the increase in the aggregate
potential function, we control the maximum of all potential functions with only a logarithmic factor loss
in the number of potential functions. This procedure can be interpreted as a multiplicative weight update
method that derandomizes the Gaussian Lipschitz concentration inequality. We note this procedure can
be used to control an arbitrary set of potential functions, not just restricted to one set of inputs. We will
elaborate more on this in the applications in Section 5.4.
Finally, we remark that we can still handle some linear subspace constraints on x in Theorem 5.1.
In
particular, we can force x to be orthogonal to a subspace of dimension r, but incur a loss of √rσ∗. This
40

means that, in many settings, we will not be able to handle Ω(n) linear constraints as in Theorem 4.2. This
is a neccesary trade-off for the two-sided approximation guarantee and arises from the non-convex nature of
the resolvent norms.

5.2
Full Spectrum and Multiplicative Weight Update Algorithm
In this subsection, we prove Theorem 5.1 assuming Proposition 5.2.
First, we restate the results from
[BBvH23] (with some modifications), which show how to control the whole spectrum by controlling the
resolvent at a finite net of points. Then, we present the multiplicative weight update algorithm to aggregate
the potential functions into one potential function. Finally, we combine these to prove Theorem 5.1.

5.4
Application: Deterministic Planted Recovery Models
In [BCSvH24], the 2-sided spectrum concentration result was used to analyze spectral algorithms for the
spiked Wigner model, which is a fundamental framework for studying low rank signal detection in high
dimensional matrices. In this model, we are given a unit vector v, and must recovery it from the noisy
observation Xθ = θvv⊤+ G, where G is a Gaussian random matrix. This model captures many well-studied
panted recovery problems, including the stochastic block model, planted clique, and Tensor PCA. A natural
recovery algorithm for the spiked Wigner model is the spectral algorithm, which estimates v from the top
eigenvector of Xθ. It is well known [BBAP05] that the performance of the spectral algorithm depends on
the sharp phase transition behavior of λmax(Xθ). In particular, with high probability,
λmax(Xθ) ≈B(θ) :=
(
2
θ ≤1
θ + 1
θ
θ ≥1.
The critical threshold is at θ = 1, below which the maximum eigenvalue of Xθ is indistinguishable from
that of G. In [BCSvH24], Bandeira et al. showed that the threshold for the spectral algorithm can be
elegantly derived by comparing the eigenvalue of Xθ with the corresponding free model and using Lehner’s
Theorem 2.24 to explicitly compute the maximum eigenvalue in the free model. Their method extends to a
broad range of models where the noise matrices can have dependent entries.

a close variant of the planted clique problem, with noise arising from G(d, 1/2) being replaced by Gaussian

hard. Such average case complexity assumptions are widely used in public key cryptography, where random
graphs/matrices are used to hide a given signal, from polynomial time detection.
Given this background, we ask the question whether we can construct pseudorandom matrices that can hide
given signals from detection by the spectral algorithm, which in many randomized settings, is conjectured to
be optimal. Our derandomization results can produce a deterministic variant of Theorem 5.8 to construct
matrices whose spectrum are close to those of random Gaussian matrices, so that they are indistinguishable
by the spectral algorithm.

## Page 58


6
Computation on Free Semicircular Matrices
We derive several formulas for computation on the space Md(C) ⊗A in this section.
In Section 6.1, we provide formulas for computing the moments of semicircular matrices and their resolvents.
This implies polynomial time algorithms for computing all potential functions used in the paper.
In Section 6.2, we prove a non-commutative “integration by parts” formula for polynomials on Md(C) ⊗A.
This will serve as a crucial tool for bounding the higher-order terms in our derandomization of the universality
results of [BvH24] in the next section. We also show that the computational formulas in Section 6.1 can be
derived conveniently from this integration by parts formula.
In Section 6.3, we present the proof of this integration by parts formula. Finally, in Section 6.4, we extend it to
the setting of rational functions, which will be required in the resolvent analysis in the proof of Theorem 1.5.
6.1
Computation of Moments and Resolvents
In this subsection, we provide formulas for computing moments and resolvents of random variables on
Md(C) ⊗A.

These formula can be derived from Lemma 2.29. For example, the first formula was used in some proofs
in Van Handel’s survey (see [vH25, Lemma 4.4]) but was not stated explicitly. In Section 6.2, we will show
that these formulas can be derived in a simple and systematic manner through the semicircular integration-
by-parts formula.
Observe that the formulas lead to a polynomial time algorithm for computing moments.
Lemma 6.2 (Efficient Computation for Moments). Let B1, B2 be arbitrary d×d matrices. For any p, q ∈N,
there is a polynomial time algorithm to compute the quantity tr ⊗τ((B1 ⊗1)Xp

free).
Proof. Note that tr ⊗τ((B1 ⊗1)Xp

free) = tr(B1 · φ[(Xfree)p(B2 ⊗1)(Xfree)q]), thus it suffices

programming algorithm to compute this quantity. Specifically, there are two types of subproblems: one of

free(B2 ⊗1)Xℓ
free]. Denote the size of each subproblem as k + ℓ.
Then we see from the recursive formulas that each subproblem only relies on solutions to subproblems of a
smaller size. The base case for the first kind of subproblem is given by φ[X0
free] = I and φ[X1
free] = A0. The
second type of subproblems eventually reduces to the first type when k or ℓreaches 0. The total number of
subproblems is at most O(pq). This leads to a polynomial time dynamic programming algorithm.

## Page 59


From Lemma 6.2, we can compute all moments of Xfree in polynomial time by simply taking B1, B2 = I.
The more general formula with arbitrary B1, B2 is used for computation with the Hessian matrix of the
potential function in Section 4.
6.1.1
From Moments to Resolvents
Now, we show how the moments of Xfree can be used to compute the moments of its resolvents. This is done
via a re-centering trick that allows us to approximate the resolvent moment by a convergent power series.

The power series leads to a polynomial time algorithm to compute the moments of the resolvent, which is
required for the algorithm of Theorem 1.3 in Section 5. We also observe that the moments of resolvent with
z > λmax + ε ∈R can be computed efficiently, which will be used in the barrier method for proving the
universality of operator norm in Section 7.
Corollary 6.4 (Efficient Computation for Resolvents). Let X ∈Md(C) ⊗A be a self-adjoint semicircular
matrix. Let z = λ + εi with ε > 0. There is a deterministic algorithm to compute tr ⊗τ(|z1 −X|−2p) up to
an additive error δ in time poly(σ(X), d, λ, p, 1/ε2, log(1/δ)).
Furthermore, the same conclusion holds when z = λmax(X) + ε ∈R.
Proof. We directly apply Lemma 6.3 with z = λ + εi and Y = λ1 −X. Note that the moments of Y can
be computed from Lemma 6.2 in polynomial time via binomial expansion, where we use the upper bound
∥Y ∥≤u = 2λ2 + 8σ(X)2 provided by Pisier’s bound in Theorem 2.23.
For the furthermore part, we write λ1 −X = ε
21 + (λ −ε
2)1 −X. By construction, Y := (λ −ε
2)1 −X has
nonnegative spectrum, with norm at most 4σ(X) + ε
2. Then, the conclusion follows from Lemma 6.3, using
Lemma 6.2 to compute the moments of Y .

6.3
Proof of Semicircular Integration-by-Parts Formula
We prove Proposition 6.5 in this subsection.
6.3.1
Intuition
To build intuition for the proof, we first give a simple combinatorial proof of (37) in the special case where
f(x) = xk is a monomial and σ2 = 1. Recall from Wick’s formula in (15) that E[xk] = |P2[k]| is exactly the
number of pair-partitions of k elements for positive integer k. We write the following recursive relation on
pairing partitions:
|P2[m + k]| =
m
X
ℓ=0
m
ℓ

· |P2[m −ℓ]| · k(k −1) · · · (k −ℓ+ 1) · |P2[k −ℓ]| · 1{ℓ≤k}.
(38)
This follows by first splitting [m + k] into a partition T1 ∪T2, where T1 contains the first m elements and
T2 contains the last k elements. Then, we condition on all the ways to split T1 into two groups, S and S,
59


## Page 63


where elements in S must pair with elements in T2 and elements in S must pair within T1. In the above
expression, ℓis the size of S,
 m


is the number of ways to choose S, and the rest of the terms count the
number of pairings given a choice of S. By noting that k(k −1) . . . (k −ℓ+ 1) · |P2[k −ℓ]| = E[f(ℓ)(x)] for
f(x) = xk, we see that (37) is equivalent to (38). By linearity, this combinatorial proof of (37) extends to
all polynomials.
While the notation in Proposition 6.5 is more complicated, the proof remains essentially the same.
In
particular, we use the fact that like how Gaussian moments count pair partitions, semicircular moments
count non-crossing pair partitions. Consider when each Fj in Proposition 6.5 is a monomial in X1, . . . , Xn.
Then we can consider the entire expression on the LHS as a monomial, which is nothing but a word over
the alphabet X1, . . . , Xn. We split the indices where Xi occur into two groups: T1 is the set of indices
corresponding to the Xi’s sandwiched between the Fj’s, while T2 is the set of indices within F1, . . . , Fm
where Xi occurs. Then, we can similarly condition on the subset S of T1 that is paired with T2. For the
remainder of this section, we first devise the notation to make this argument formal, then we extend from
monomials to arbitrary polynomials by linearity.
6.3.2
Moments of Monomials
Let T be an arbitrary index set. Consider a set of random variables {Xi | i ∈T } where each Xi ∈Md(C)⊗A.
We formally define the notion of monomials over these variables.
Definition 6.7 (Monomials). A monomial of degree k over the variables {Xi | i ∈T } is a polynomial of
the form (B1 ⊗1)Xi1 · · · (Bk ⊗1) · Xik · (Bk+1 ⊗1), where each Bj ∈Md(C). For ease of notation, we use
the shorthand B1Xi1 · · · BkXikBk+1 to denote this monomial.
Note that the degree is k because the Xij’s are variables and Bj’s are coefficients. Identify each monomial
with a word W over the alphabet C := {Xi : i ∈T } ∪Md(C).
From this definition, it is clear that the set of all monomials form a basis for the vector space of polynomials
over the variables {Xi | i ∈T } with Md(C)-valued coefficients. Moreover, we see that the expressions on
the left and right hand sides of Proposition 6.5 are multi-linear in the polynomials F1, . . . , Fm. Thus, by
expanding out, it suffices to prove Proposition 6.5 in the case where each Fj is a monomial. Henceforth, we
assume that each Fj is a monomial.
Now, we define the notion of indexing and applying the functionals φπ in Definition 2.16 on the monomials.
We remark that the φπ functionals, used for the free Wick formula in Theorem 2.28, are crucial for the
“conditioning step” later in the proof.
Definition 6.8 (Linear Functional on Words). Consider a monomial word
W = B1Xi1 · · · BkXikBk+1.

where again we use BiXi to denote (Bi ⊗1)Xi. We say that π ∼W if W[j] = W[j′] for all (j, j′) ∈π.

Let WC := W1XiW2Xi · · · WmXi be the “combined” monomial with degree k := k1 + · · · + km + m. Since
the variables X1, . . . , Xn are centered and freely independent, it follows from Lemma 2.29 that

6.3.3
Character Substitutions
Now, we will show how the conditioning formula in (40) can be interpreted in terms of derivatives. The
main operation to introduce is the character substitution operation, which is a linear map that takes one
monomial to another by replacing one variable with another.
Definition 6.9 (Substitution). Let W be a monomial of degree k. Given characters H1, H2, . . . , Hℓ∈C
and a subset of indices S = {1 ≤j(1) < j(2) < . . . < j(ℓ) ≤k}, we define W[H1, H2, . . . , Hℓ; S] as
the word obtained by starting from W and replacing the characters in S with H1, . . . , Hℓ.
Formally, if
W = B1Xi1 · · · BkXikBk+1, then W[H1, H2, . . . , Hℓ; S] = B1Y1 · · · BkYkBk+1 where

of Equation 39, conditioning on π ≤ξ is equivalent to substituting the copies of Xi in each part of ξ with a
distinct freely independent copy of Xi.

Deterministic Universality
In this section, we prove Theorem 1.4 and Theorem 1.5 and show applications in deterministic constructions
of expander graphs.

Organization: Since the proofs are quite technically involved, we first present some interesting applications
of our main results to constructing expander graphs in Section 7.2. Then, we provide a technical overview
of the proofs in Section 7.3. The proof of Theorem 7.2 will be presented in Section 7.4, and the proof of
Theorem 7.3 will be given in Section 7.5.
7.2
Deterministic Expander Constructions
In this subsection, we present our results for explicit constructions of near Ramanujan graphs in the mod-
erately dense to dense regime. While many probabilistic constructions of expanders yield (nearly) optimal
spectral gap [Fri08, Bor20, CGVTvH25, HMY25], a fundamental question in theoretical computer science
is whether these constructions can be made explicit, or polynomial time deterministic. Explicit construc-
tion of expander graphs is an important and well-studied topic, with applications in algorithm design and
complexity theory.
Previously, Marcus, Spielman and Srivastava [MSS18] showed the existence of bipartite Ramanujan graphs for
all degrees and sizes, and their construction was made polynomial time and deterministic by Cohen [Coh16].
However, in the non-bipartite setting, much less is known about deterministic constructions compared to
probabilistic constructions. In particular, all previous efficient deterministic constructions of near-Ramanujan
non-bipartite graphs are restricted to the constant degree regime [MOP20, OW20, JMO+22], and not much
is known when the degree is at least polylogarthimically large.
Using the derandomization results in this paper, we obtain deterministic constructions of (not necessarily
bipartite) near-Ramanujan graphs in this regime for several random models, including the random edge-
signing model in Section 7.2.1, the random permutation model in Section 7.2.2, and two random lift models
in Section 7.2.3.
The following corollary of Theorem 7.3 is used in this subsection for ease of applications. The proof follows
from Theorem 7.3 by applying Pisier’s result in Theorem 2.23 and triangle inequality to bound the norm of
the free model.

7.2.1
Random Signings
The first model that we will derandomize is the random signing of regular graphs. Given a k-regular graph
G over d vertices, the goal is to find a signing of its edges so that its signed adjacency matrix has small
operator norm.
This is an important model for expander construction due to its connection to 2-lifts, which will be elaborated
more in Section 7.2.3. The random signing model is a canonical example of the “sparse Wigner model” [vH17].
It is well known [BvH16] that if k is at least Ω(polylog d), then a random signing of its adjacency matrix
has norm at most 2
√
k · (1 + od(1)). To our knowledge, there is no known polynomial time algorithm to
deterministically construct such a signing in this regime, and we present such a deterministic algorithm.
Theorem 7.5 (Deterministic Edge Signing). Let G = ([d], E) be a k-regular graph over d vertices with
adjacency matrix A. If k ≳log4 d, then there is a polynomial time deterministic algorithm to find a signing
x ∈{−1, 1}|E|, so that the signed adjacency matrix A(x) satisfies

To remove the log d factor, we decompose G into the union of at most k + 1 edge-disjoint matchings

7.2.2
Random Permutations
The next model that we will derandomize is the construction of regular expanders through the union of ran-
dom perfect matchings. This is one of the most common and natural models for generating uniform random
regular graphs. The classic result of Friedman [Fri08] showed that for any constant k and d large enough,
random k-regular graphs sampled from this model have spectral radius at most 2
√
k −1 + od(1) with high
probability. In [BvH16, Theorem 3.8], it was shown that the near-Ramanujan property in this model contin-
ues to hold when k ≳log4 d, which was the first time such a result was proven for any k = ω(log d/ log log d).
Our derandomization of their concentration inequality gives the following expilcit construction result.

ministic algorithm to construct a 2k-regular graph over d vertices (possibly with parallel edges and self loops),

Proof: While we used pairwise independent bits for the random edge signing model to ensure polynomial
support size, we do not know how to obtain polynomial support size with pairwise independent permutations.
Fortunately, an approximate version of pairwise independent random permutation model is known in the
literature to have polynomial support size, which we will need to use for the proceeding applications.
Definition 7.7 (Approximate Pairwise Uniform Permutations). A distribution D over Pm is called (δ, q)-
wise uniform if for every sequence (i1, . . . , iq) with ij ∈[m], the distribution of (σ(i1), . . . , σ(iq)) has total
variation distance at most δ from the uniform distribution on [m]q.
Theorem 7.8 (Approximate Pairwise Uniform Permutations [Kas07, KNR09]). For any m, q, δ > 0, there
exists a (δ, q)-wise uniform distribution over Pm whose support has size poly(mq, 1
δ ) and can be computed in
time poly(mq, 1
δ ).
In the following claim, we show that replacing uniform permutations in a random matrix model with ap-
proximate pairwise uniform permutations does not alter the parameters σ2 and ν2 by much.

7.2.3
Random Lifts
A more general model of constructing expanders is by starting with a well-expanding base graph and taking
a large lift of the base graph.
Definition 7.10 (Lift of Graphs). Let G = ([d0], E) be a graph. A graph H = ([d0] × [m], E′) is an m-lift
of G if its edge set is comprised of a set of matchings where for each (u, v) ∈E, H has a perfect matching
between {u} × [m] and {v} × [m].
Generally, we can take lifts of graphs with multi-edges or self loops. In this view, the permutation model is
simply a d-lift of a graph with a single vertex and k self-loops. However, we restrict our attention to simple
base graphs for sake of simplicity.

In Section 7.2.1, we studied random edge signings of a regular graph.
A key reason why this model is
important is that the eigenvalues of the signed adjacency matrix of the base graph are exactly the new
eigenvalues introduced by a corresponding 2-lift [BL06]. Thus, Theorem 7.5 implies that for any k-regular
graph G with degree Ω(log4 d), we can deterministically computes a near-Ramanujan 2-lift of G.
Previous Work: Bordenave [Bor20] showed that for any fixed k-regular base graph G, a random m-lift of
G is a 2
√
k −1 + O( log log m
log m ) near-Ramanujan lift with high probability, as long as k ≲
log m
log log m. Mohanty,
O’Donnell, and Paredes [MOP20] gave a derandomization of Bordenave’s result by replacing a single and
arbitrarily large lift with an arbitrarily long sequence of smaller lifts. In particular, they start by applying
Bordenave’s method on a small enough lift size that can be derandomized by “brute-force” using (δ, q)-wise
uniform permutations. Then, they take iterated 2-lifts of this “seed graph”, which are derandomized using
(δ, q)-wise independent bits, for q on the order of
√
k log d. This was a significant improvement of a similar
approach by Bilu and Linial [BL06]. We note that these constructions in [MOP20] have runtime on the order
of dO(
√
k) and so they are only in polynomial time for constant k.
Brailovskaya and van Handel [BvH24] showed that the sharp matrix concentration inequalities can be applied
in the complementary regime of k ≥polylog m to construct near-Ramanujan lifts. The proof of Bordenave
does not work in this regime because it requires conditioning on large neighborhoods of each vertex having
at most one cycle, which no longer holds with high probability in the dense setting.
Our Work: By derandomizing the concentration inequalities in [BvH24], we obtain explicit constructions
of near-Ramanujan lifts when k ≳log6 d0 · log4 d in polynomial time. In fact, we will derandomize two more
general models, both of which will capture this result as a special case. The first model we derandomize is
of lifting graphs with arbitrary degrees. The second model we derandomize is applying group-based lifts to
regular graphs. We will present the precise results in the next two subsubsections.
General Near-Ramanujan Lifts
The notion of Ramanujan graphs can be generalized to irregular graphs, by comparing the spectrum of a
graph with that of its universal cover tree T(G).
Definition 7.11 (Non-Regular Ramanujan Graphs). Let G be a (possibly irregular) graph and AT(G) be
the infinite dimensional adjacency operator of its universal cover tree. Then G is called Ramanujan if its
non-trivial eigenvalues are bounded in absolute value by
AT(G)
.
If G is k-regular, then T(G) is the infinite k-regular tree, and it is well known that
AT(G)
 = 2
√
k −1.
If G is an irregular graph with maximum degree kmax, then √kmax ≤
AT(G)
 ≤2√kmax −1, where the
upper bound is due to T(G) being a subgraph of the kmax-regular infinite tree, and the lower bound is due
to the star graph over kmax + 1 vertices being a subgraph of T(G). Since the work of Friedman [Fri08], the
construction of irregular near-Ramanujan graphs has been a long standing question.
Bordenave and Collins [BC19] resolved this question by showing that if H is a random m-lift of a base
graph G, then the set of new eigenvalues of H are bounded by
AT(G)
 + om(1) with high probability, as
long as the size of the base graph is at most O(log m/ log log m). O’Donnell and Wu [OW20] provided a

to hold in the complementary m ≳log4(md0) regime, and we derandomize their result.

Group-Based Lifts
Another model of lifts that have recently garnered attention is the group-based lift, which can be viewed as
a generalization of Cayley graphs.
Definition 7.16 (Group-Based Lifts). Let G = ([d0], E) be a graph. Let Γ be a finite group of size m.
Let H = ([d0] × Γ, E′) be an m-lift of G. We say that H is a Γ-lift if for each e = uv ∈E, there is a
group element he ∈Γ such that the matching between {u} × Γ and {v} × Γ in H is given by the edge set
{(u, α), (v, heα) : α ∈Γ}.
In [ACKM19], it was shown that if G is a k regular λ-spectral expander over d0 vertices with k ≲
p
d0/ log d0
and Γ is the abelian group Z/mZ, then a uniform random group lift of size m has spectral radius at most
O(λ). This result was crucially used in the construction of quantum and classical LDPC codes in [PK22].
Jeronmino, Mitall, Paredes, O’Donnell, and Tulsiani [JMO+22] gave explicit constructions of group-based
lifts of k-regular graphs for constant k, and gave explicit constructions of quantum and classical LDPC codes
as a consequence.
In particular, in the regime where m ≤exp(O( ε2 log d0
k
)), their algorithm constructs deterministic Abelian
lifts of the base graph G whose spectral radius is bounded by 2
√
k −1 + ε. Their methods are extensions of
those in [MOP20], and in the regime where k is poly-logarithmic in md0, are no longer of polynomial time
nor providing optimal bounds. The results in [BvH24] naturally capture the complementary dense regime for
random group-lifts. Our algorithms give the following deterministic construction of near-Ramanujan group
lifts in this regime. We note that our bounds do not require Γ to be Abelian (or have any specific structure).
73

The key in the derandomization process is to select an appropriate it and Z′

Therefore, the main task is to control the expected potential under a random perturbation Zi ⊗1 −Xi for
i ∈It. A natural idea would be using a similar analysis as in Section 4 and Section 5 to bound the change
of potential. However, there are two differences in the iterative swapping algorithm that require new ideas.
For the first one, bounding the perturbations in Section 4 and Section 5 can be naturally reinterpreted as
taking the difference between a finite dimensional random update √ηA(y) ⊗1 and an update with √η ¯X′
free,
a free copy of ¯Xfree = Xfree −A0 ⊗1. However, in the present setting, we are forced to directly work with
the update Zi ⊗1 −Xi, where the Xi term is not free from our current solution Xf,t. As long as this Xi is
present in our update, we cannot directly apply the crucial tool Lemma 2.30 to analyze the second order error
term. To overcome this issue, we will use the “semicircular integration-by-parts” formula in Proposition 6.5
to expand the perturbation error terms into a series of terms where we replace the occurrences of Xi with
free copies of it, so as to control the error terms.
For the second one, the perturbation sizes in Section 4 and Section 5 are controlled by the step length η,
which could be made arbitrarily small if necessary (but with the trade-off of increasing the running time).
Because of this, it suffices to estimate the potential function with a second-order approximation and bound
all the higher order error terms with a naive worst case bound via Lemma 3.20. However, the perturbation
Zi ⊗1 −Xi in this section may not be small enough in the worst case, and so using a worst case bound to
control the higher order error terms would not work anymore. To overcome this, we follow a similar idea as
in [BvH24, Proposition 5.1] to control the higher order error terms in expectation via an infinite dimensional
trace inequality in Proposition 7.18, but this leads to even more involved calculations than those in Section 4
and Section 5.

adjoint random matrices such that maxi ∥Zi∥≤ρ with probability one and ∥Pn

argument is quite different. The form in the LHS of our expression arises from the Taylor expansion of the
potential update formula in the random-swap algorithm. The Zi and Xi terms arise from the update Zi −Xi
corresponding to applying the swap to index i. Crucially, we use the simple observation that if ∥Zi∥≤ρ
then ∥Xi∥≤2ρ by Lemma 2.32. If we replaced Xi in the above bound with finite dimensional Gi, where Gi
is a Gaussian matrix with the same covariance profile as Zi, then the bound above does not hold anymore
since the spectrum of Gi is not bounded almost surely. We will provide a full proof of Proposition 7.18 in
Section 7.6 for completeness.

Deterministic Moment Universality

where F(Xf,t, Xi, Zi ⊗1) is a multivariate non-commutative monomial in Xf,t, Xi, and Zi ⊗1. Brailovskaya
and van Handel [BvH24] provided a trace inequality ([BvH24, Proposition 5.1], which plays a key role in
their work) to control a similar quantity

where Aij’s are a family of (possibly dependent) random matrices with identical distribution for each fixed i,
and B1, . . . , Bk is another family of (possibly dependent) random matrices that are independent from Aij’s.
This is essentially what we need, but with two issues.
• The first one is that their inequality is for finite dimensional random matrices. We need to use a version
for infinite dimensional operators, i.e., Proposition 7.18.
• The second one is a more serious issue. In our setting, the monomial F(Xf,t, Xi, Zi ⊗1) has two types
of semicircular matrices that are not freely independent. In particular, Xf,t contains an Xi that is
to be removed, thus it is not freely independent from Xi. This issue is handled via the “semiciricular
integration by parts” formula in Proposition 6.5, which replaces these “dependent” Xi’s with freely
independent copies of Xi, but requires expanding out into more higher-order terms.8.
8We note that in the interpolation argument of [BvH24], applying the main trace inequality, proposition 5.1, also required
a similar decoupling procedure, which was achieved using cumulant expansion on the Zi’s instead.

However, the dependence on ∥A0 ⊗1 + Pn

strong enough for some applications such as constructing near-Ramanujan graphs.
To capture the spectral edge behavior, our idea is to use the barrier method developed by Batson, Spielman,
Srivastava [BSS12], where we shift a barrier λt ∈R by δt > 0 in each iteration such that λt > λmax(Xf,t)
through the whole iterative swapping process. Then, in the end, λn serves as an upper bound on λmax(Xf,n)
(note that it suffices to control λmax in order to control the operator norm, see Section 7.5). As discussed
in the introduction, the barrier method only works for the free model but not for the Gaussian model as its
maximum eigenvalue is not bounded.
The key in this algorithm is to select a good swap so that the shift of barrier δt is small in each iteration.
However, it is difficult to control the spectral edge λmax directly as it is not a smooth quantity. Thus, we
control the alternative potential function, the 2p-th moment of the resolvent, i.e., tr ⊗τ((λt(I⊗1)−Xf,t)−2p)
for some p = Θ(log d). We show that if the potential value is bounded by (2ε)−2p, then the barrier λt ≥
λmax(Xf,t) + ε (see Lemma 7.22).
Therefore, we reduce the problem to selecting an appropriate swap together with an appropriate shifting δt
so that the potential value remains bounded. It turns out that, if we choose a random swap Zi ⊗1−Xi, then
the key in controlling the shifting amount is in controlling the expected change of potential (see Lemma 7.24)
after the swap in the new barrier position λ = λ + δt, i.e.,
Ei,Zi

The analysis of the expected change of potential in Lemma 7.24 is the most technical part, where we will
have similar issues as in the deterministic moments universality analysis but with more complicated moments
of resolvents. The proof will follow a similar framework as in the analysis of potential change for moments
in Section 7.4 and combine with some analytical ideas in Section 5.
7.4
Deterministic Moment Universality
We start this section by formally describing the Iterative Swapping Algorithm for Theorem 7.2.
Iterative Swapping Algorithm for Moments
• Initialize: I0 ←[n] and Xf,0 ←A0 ⊗1 + Pn
i=1 Xi;
• For t = 0 to n −1 do
1. Find i∗∈It and Z′ ∈supp(Zi∗) which minimizes
∥Xf,t + Z′ ⊗1 −Xi∗∥2p.
2. Update Xf,t+1 ←Xf,t + Z′ ⊗1 −Xi∗and It+1 ←It\{i∗}.
• Return φ[Xf,n].

In order to achieve λn > λmax(Xf,n) after the last iteration, we maintain two invariants throughout the whole
process:
1. The barrier λt is well above the spectral edge of Xf,t, such that λt ≥λmax(Xf,t) + ε;
2. The potential value is bounded, such that tr ⊗τ(Mλt(Xf,t))2p ≤(2ε)−2p.
Observe that the two invariants are satisfied at the beginning of the algorithm, due to our choice of λ0 =
λmax(Xf,0) + 2ε. Thus, to maintain the second invariant, it suffices to ensure that the potential value never
increases.

stronger than what we need. However, to ensure the second invariant can be satisfied by the update, we
need λt to be bounded away from spec(Xf,t). In turn, the following lemma shows that the second invariant
also helps to maintain the first invariant.

7.6
An Infinite Dimensional Trace Inequality
Brailovskaya and van Handel [BvH24, Proposition 5.1] proved a trace inequality to control a higher-order
quantity of the following form

n

i=1

,
(67)
where Aij’s are a family of (possibly dependent) random matrices with identical distribution for each fixed i,
and B1, . . . , Bk is another family of (possibly dependent) random matrices that are independent from Aij’s.
As mentioned in Section 1.3, when analyzing the swapping algorithm for derandomizing the general random
matrix model, we need to bound the expected change of the potential function under a random swap
perturbation of the form Zi ⊗1 −Xi, where Zi is a random matrix and Xi is an infinite dimensional
semicircular matrix. The expectation of higher-order error terms under the perturbation is in a similar form
as in (67), but involves infinite dimensional semicircular matrix Xi. Therefore, we need to derive an infinite
dimensional variant of the trace inequality in [BvH24, Proposition 5.1] (see Section 7.3 for more details on
the motivations).
